Use FIFO — oldest batch consumed first."*), i.e. **undecided**. `grep -rn "record.*batch\|which batch was\|sold.*batch" features/03-*.md features/26-*.md features/34-*.md` -> **no output** = **no existing feature records the batch on the sale**. **⚠️ THIS IS WORSE THAN THE `qty_remaining` DEFECT (feature 374), BECAUSE THE RULE IT VIOLATES IS THE RULE THE FEATURE PIPELINE IS ABOUT TO WRITE CODE AGAINST**: the moment a selling path lands it must answer "which batch?", and the plausible wrong answer is the quiet one *("there's only one active batch per item today, so it's unambiguous")* — **true today, and it stops being true the moment the cook makes more tiramisu at 3pm**. The consequence is **NOT a crash**: the ledger and the order simply disagree, the ledger's own structure looks intact (both FKs non-null, both pointing at real rows), and **an owner's stock number drifts by one unit with no way to tell which sale was wrong — a SILENT historical mis-attribution with no ground truth to audit against**. **Cheap now (one nullable column), expensive after (a full historical audit)**. **⭐ THE METHOD IS NOW THREE-FOR-THREE**: feature 374 (`ramaraihan12/warung-pos` → *"Stock is never a stored number"*) → feature 379 (`sajeercherukottayil/Ordova` + `boethius68/KitchenLink` → *"who did what, when, from what state"*) → feature 385 (`kodlyft/xpos` → *"select specific batch/serial numbers during sale"*). **Three external READMEs, three first-party findings, all in `backend/app/models.py`, all found in ~90 seconds of `grep` once an external artifact gave a reason to look. Ninety citations had not found the first one.
